Given the compute cost seems manageable but the decision volume isn't, I think the right call is to flag this as blocked and propose a smaller band (like the 4,655-type / 95% set) rather than attempt full coverage.

For the sparse tail (8,969 types, 1.6 sites/type), defining a struct per 1-2 site local is just noise — I'd propose a residual cause like `TYPE-SPARSE` for anything under 3 sites, treating it as a policy decision rather than gaming the metric. But the real open question is who gets to decide "fits the phase" when the blocked/go call depends on T2's judgment about whether the projected numbers are acceptable.

Looking at scope, I'm checking whether some later task handles the long tail, but I don't have visibility into other tasks. For TNC 0 itself, roughly 13.5k type decisions plus two header batches works out to about 20-25 hours of sweep time plus 2 hours of residue — compute-feasible if the developer accepts a day of work, since most decisions are mechanical (my rule auto-resolved 57/74, with the 17 overrides being entity folds that rename rather than recount). Scaling the mechanical "define" rate up, I'm estimating around 9% duplicate classes from coincident twins, which projects to roughly 1,200 dup classes across the full 13.5k definitions.

Thinking through whether defining all 13,661 types is actually sound, I'm worried the tail — about 8,969 types at 1.6 sites each — would add ~9k mostly one-field placeholder struct definitions across ~14.7k sites, hurting readability. That pushes me toward recommending a 95% coverage band instead of full coverage; recalculating, 95% of current distribution lands at 7,083 types, and I'm cross-checking that against the plan's earlier "4,655 types covers 95%" figure from the original 291,819-site baseline, now that we've cleared about 145k sites.

Since the task's done-when criteria (unbacked 0, check-structs passing) still need finishing, and there's no review flag on this entry, I'm leaning toward letting c4 finish running before returning anything — the final status will be either done or blocked, and my judgment points toward blocked with a band recommendation since full TNC 0 doesn't fit this phase. I still want to pin down the remaining types' sweep more precisely by computing the union of bodies across bands.

I'm projecting the full upper bound at roughly 29,461 at 2.57s each, which works out to about 21 hours of compute, plus redraw passes — a phase that runs in full with no cap could absorb that if the developer's okay with it. The real cost isn't the compute though, it's decision quality: the entity checks I did by reading each type individually won't scale mechanically across 13.5k types the same way, though the top-75 sample showed about 9% entity folds, which gives some sense of what's unmeasurable at this scale. My recommendation is leaning toward a band of types with at least 3 TNC sites, and I'm working out that exact count now — landing around 13,624.

Breaking this down further, types with 3+ sites split into roughly 6,330 types holding about 144,399 sites (94% of the remaining pool), while types with only 1-2 sites amount to 7,294 types covering 9,185 sites — these sparse cases seem to stem from placeholder structs that cost more to carry than they're worth defining. So the next task would be to fold/define the ≥3-site types (6,330 types, ~144k sites, roughly a 20-hour sweep with maybe 4 header batches), and stamp the 1-2 site tail as TYPE-SPARSE, leaving it to the developer to decide whether that residual is acceptable as a scope/product policy. I'm also noting that the ≥3 threshold can include single-body types that happen to hit exactly 3 sites, which is fine. Now I'm waiting on the c4 numbers before finalizing, so I'll start drafting the summary skeleton with the policy framing and fill in the numbers once they're ready.

If the census snapshot changed after restamp but before the later diagnostic check, that would explain why some bodies labeled t3_S1/TNC appear untouched — their live cause computed now differs from what it was when restamp actually ran.

I want to verify this by rerunning restamp_rows directly against the current (post-regeneration) census for the ov P,X sites to see if they'd move now — if so, that confirms the fix is reordering steps so census regeneration happens before restamp, not some pairing bug in the residue_cause logic itself.

The restamp function is confirmed working, leaving zero unprocessed after a full pass—the earlier "miss" was just census staleness, since restamp read a sites.jsonl snapshot taken before the sweep's final batches completed, not an actual pairing bug. It's now 10:35 and the census itself dates to 10:24.

Rather than a pairing fix, I'm thinking the right response to the expert is to confirm there's no bug in restamp_rows, but still propose a cheap staleness guard: compare sites.jsonl's mtime against the max mtime of source files in scope, and have restamp refuse to run if the census predates recent changes. That seems like a proportional safeguard against this exact recurrence.

I need to apply the same guard to residue, since it also relies on census and would produce wrong uncovered entries on a stale snapshot. I'll factor a small helper that checks staleness given paths, and note that the step ordering in the brief (residue, restamp, then census) needs to run census first when the guard is in place — which is actually the more correct order anyway. For the selftest, I can simulate staleness by touching a file newer than the census path and confirming the guard trips.

That said, mtime-based staleness detection risks false positives: operations like make clean or extract-all might touch src file mtimes without changing content, which would trip the guard every time even when nothing substantive changed. A content-hash comparison would be safer but requires knowing whether the census already records per-file hashes, which I'm not sure about — checking all residue candidates against hashes would also be costly. Given the proportionality of the ask, I think the right move is to report that there's no actual restamp_rows bug, fix the ordering so census runs before residue/restamp (and rerun after), and just recommend a staleness guard as a future improvement rather than implementing a risky one now.

I'll skip the guard for now and just report it. Let me wait for the cycle to finish, monitoring the background process via its PID.

Understood. T2 stays paused and I won't resume it on any more notices.

Restarting Claude Code is safe:
- **What stops:** the restart ends the expert and coder that are still alive.
- **What keeps running:** the detached restruct cycle survives the restart, finishes its current batch and commits it. That batch has to pass the usual 218/218 byte-identical check.
- **What's already saved:** all 38 source batches and the T2.c3 and T2.c4 commits.

After the restart and the mod update, once you want the work to go on, I'll restart T2 fresh with a note on where it stands. The new expert reads the T2 log, checks whether the cycle has finished, and goes on from there.

It's waiting on a CPU-bound job, not doing work of its own. That coder (T2.c4) started a second restruct cycle, a re-draw over the types that still have residue, and is waiting for it to finish. The cycle is what's using the CPU.

- **Progress:** the re-draw's first batch committed at 10:46. It turned 800 cast sites into struct members and passed the usual 218/218 check. The second batch is compiling now, with its 50 file edits in progress.
- **Size:** the re-draw covers about 259 files, roughly 6 batches of 50, at about 10 to 20 minutes each. That's roughly an hour more if it runs to the end.
- **After a restart:** the cycle is detached, so it keeps going and committing batches through a Claude Code restart. Only the waiting coder goes away.

If you want the CPU quiet before the update, I can stop the cycle by its process id once the current batch commits, so nothing is left half-written. Otherwise letting it finish is harmless, and the fresh T2 expert after the restart will pick up its commits.

I'm leaving the queue held for now, because releasing it could start the next task (T12) while you want things paused.

## 141655-046r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-15T20:16:55.090Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f

---

The PA3 update you were waiting for has arrived. Please restart Claude Code now: one of its settings only takes effect after a restart, and the restart also loads the new mod.

What it changes, in plain terms:
- **Restarts no longer pause tasks.** If Claude Code restarts, updates or the terminal closes while a task runs, the task's expert picks up by itself at the next start, and I tell you in one line. Only your own Ctrl+C waits for your go. This should end T2's run of false stop notices.
- **Long jobs run detached.** A long command such as a restruct cycle runs as a tracked job. The agent hands back "waiting on job" and stops, and the mod wakes it when the job ends, instead of the agent sitting in a waiting tool call. Pending jobs survive a restart.
- **The talk hold is tighter.** Only your messages hold the queue while we talk, and the hold ends 15 minutes after you last spoke.
- **Idle agents are handled better.** Waiting agents are kept warm by pings sized to them. Nothing is sent to an agent whose cache has gone cold; a fresh agent of the same kind takes the message instead.
- **Agents at their context limit** finish and commit only working code, save any unfinished edit as a patch, and hand off cleanly.
- **Smaller fixes:**
  - installing a new phase plan keeps finished tasks finished;
  - the mod's own commits get five minutes instead of one, and a leftover git lock is cleared automatically;
  - a running session checks for new releases every 8 hours and asks you once per version whether to install after the current task.

After the restart, T2 should resume on its own. If it doesn't, tell me and I'll restart it fresh from its log. The restruct cycle that was running stays committed batch by batch.

The work for T2 is finished and committed (63fc7e8bf6). I'm returning blocked only because the plan's judgment clause says to when the projection shows TNC 0 does not fit the phase, and it doesn't.

What T2 did:
- Each of the top 75 TNC map types now has a decision in config/type_folds.tsv: 51 written as canonical defs, 7 folded onto existing types, 16 left residual with a named cause. Unkstruct_80126B58 was already canonical; its TNC count was 3,457 stale stamps, which the restamp cleared.
- The defs went in two header batches in include/struct_types.h. Both pass, since HEAD includes them and the clean verify gate on HEAD is green.
- The bodies were re-swept by rung S and their residue rows rewritten.

This attempt finished three things the killed coder left open:
- check-structs: 5 new dup classes were coincident layouts. `struct_twins.py` was reading the stale P38 census, so I regenerated docs/struct-twins.md from P37. check-structs is now OK: 0 dup, 0 dead, 0 stale.
- check-residue: the 124 bodies that the redraw batches rS1..rS3 rewrote had no residue rows. I wrote them; check-residue on ov_* now shows 0 uncovered.
- Redraw loop: batches rS4..rS21 were re-planning the same 50 files with 0 sites each time. No live process was left.
- Verify gate passed in full: check-structs OK, check-residue 0, then clean, extract and check-all at 218 passed, 0 failed of 218.

Numbers:

| Measure | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| TNC sites | 291,819 | 139,520 (−52%) |
| TNC types | 13,736 | 12,742 |
| Raw P | 257,470 | 174,987 |
| Raw I | 49,831 | 48,409 |
| Raw X | 13,790 | 12,031 |
| Raw M | 15,128 | 5,587 |

- Uncovered and unbacked are 0 in every form and segment.
- 90,337 sites went to MEMBER and 15,466 to KEPT.
- The biggest new or grown causes are NO-EDIT 29,684, NO-FIELD 9,133, GADDR 7,285, TYPE-UNION 6,472 and TYPE-NO-LAYOUT 4,131.
- About 9.6k of the NO-EDIT count belongs to the 7 TYPE-NO-MEMBER types. They were stamped while their defs still existed, and `--restamp` only rewrites TNC stamps, so those sites are covered but under the wrong cause.
- Header fan-out: batch 1 took 115 s to pass the gate. For batch 2, touching include/struct_types.h and running the full check took 51 s and rebuilt 4,555 objects, so the old 6-minute estimate no longer holds.
- Sweep cost: 4.48 min per 1k TNC sites removed, or 2.57 s per body.

Projection for the remaining 12,742 types (the plan's 13,661 came from the census before T2):

| Band | Types | Sites | Sweep time |
|---|---|---|---|
| 50% | 216 | ~69.8k | ~5.2 h |
| 80% | 1,834 | ~111.6k | ~8.3 h |
| 95% | 6,676 | ~132.5k | ~9.9 h |
| TNC 0 | 12,742 | 139,520 | ~10.4 h |

The tail past 95% is 6,066 types holding about 7k sites, roughly one site each. Sweep time is not what limits this; the decisions are. T2's 75 rows needed hand evidence. TNC 0 would mean about 12.7k decision rows and about 8.7k new canonical structs, many of them for a single site.

Decision for you: which band the next task covers. I recommend the 95% band, with decisions made by tooling:
- define by default under an address name;
- residual by layout class: conflicts give TYPE-UNION, size 0 gives TYPE-NO-LAYOUT, one field gives TYPE-SCALAR, negative offsets give TYPE-INTERIOR;
- fold only when lineage, a shared global anchor, or field-wise copies are detected.

Add a one-time restamp that also fixes the ~9.6k NO-EDIT stamps on residual types. Leave the ~7k-site tail residual under a new single-use cause rather than TNC.

The fold policy is written as `binding:` decisions in the summary: entity versus coincident layout, the canonical name and home file, when a type stays residual and with which cause, and the batch order. Two tool gotchas are recorded there too: `struct_twins.py` defaults to the stale P38 census, and restruct_cycle.sh has no exit when a batch does nothing.

T2 needs your decision, because the answer changes the 3.40 milestone.

T2 finished its pilot, and all of its work is committed (63fc7e8bf6) with every gate green. It reported "blocked" only because the plan told it to stop if the full goal looked too large for one phase.

**What the pilot did.** It made a decision for each of the 75 struct types that held the most residue: 51 got a canonical definition, 7 were merged into types we already had, and 16 stay residue with a named reason. That cut the "type not canonical" residue roughly in half, from 291,819 to 139,520 sites. Raw casts fell by about 94,000 along the way. One struct had looked odd: it carried 3,457 residue labels left over from before it was defined, and those are now cleared.

**The numbers for the rest.** About 12,700 types are left, and the residue is very lopsided:

| Coverage | Types to decide | Sites cleared | Sweep time |
|---|---|---|---|
| 80% | 1,834 | ~112k | ~8 h |
| 95% | 6,676 | ~132k | ~10 h |
| 100% (literal 0) | ~12,700 | ~140k | ~10.4 h |

The compile sweeps are cheap. The real cost is the decisions: reaching zero means about 8,700 new struct definitions. About 6,000 of the last types cover only around one site each, so most of those would be one-off structs that exist only to satisfy the counter.

**My recommendation is the 95% band.** Decide those 6,676 types with rules applied automatically by tooling, which T2 has already worked out. The roughly 7,000 tail sites stay residue under a reason of their own, such as "single-use type", instead of "not canonical".

That needs your explicit agreement to change the milestone wording, from "TYPE-NOT-CANONICAL gone" to "gone above the 95% band, with the tail ledgered under its own reason". Without that agreement it would just be renaming the problem so the gate passes. The tail is the same kind of residue the plan already accepts for other causes.

Going for a literal zero is possible, but it spends most of the remaining effort on thousands of near-empty structs, which hurts readability rather than helping it.

I'm noticing the hash names have drifted — most T2 hash keys don't appear in the current struct_map, since the census likely named clusters differently than the canonical names defined later in the header. I need to check whether the type names in the residue census actually match these map types.

Since residue_census.json and struct_map were written at different times, they may come from different census runs, so I should just regenerate everything fresh — a full type_census with --check-casts --residue on HEAD, now that T2 is committed, plus tnc_types added.

Looking at T2's calibration for TYPE-UNION: cases with width conflicts or overlap-only conflicts get flagged, but some folded/canonical-looking structs also show big conflicts despite being defined as cf=0 in T2. So the actual binding rule seems to be simply "conflicts → TYPE-UNION," which is a blunt policy but apparently the one being applied.

I need to distinguish sign-only conflicts (same width, different sign) from true width conflicts — sign-only shouldn't trigger a union since that's handled by sign macros, while overlap entries (like a 4-byte field at offset 0 overlapping a 1-byte at offset 3) do need a union, matching how T2 treated 801345F8. So the precise rule is: TYPE-UNION only if a conflict entry is an overlap or involves more than one distinct width, not just a sign mismatch.

Then for ordering the residual checks: size_hint of 0 means TYPE-NO-LAYOUT (as with 57db4d07, which had negative offsets and overlaps but T2 still called it NO-LAYOUT), negative offsets alone signal TYPE-INTERIOR (like b6c7c603), unions come next, and a single field maps to TYPE-SCALAR. I'm realizing that since type_census.json only keeps the top 300 names by count, any listed rows beyond that cutoff get dropped by struct_twins' load_rows, causing their sections to disappear and re-trigger gating each run. Since I can't modify type_census, the fix needs to live in load_rows itself—preserving rows currently listed under `## Twins` even if they fall outside the top-300 json and aren't in dup_gating.tsv.

I need to confirm these surviving rows are genuinely valid tier-2 matches rather than stale: a row absent from dup_gating was presumably cleared by twins_ok, but if its listed lhash is now stale, keeping it would just preserve outdated data. So I want to check whether type_census or check-structs records stale lhashes anywhere in its output, since dup_gating.tsv alone doesn't distinguish "valid non-gating" from "stale non-gating" rows.

Found the root cause: `type_census.json` only retains the top 300 `dup_layout_classes`, so when struct_twins' `load_rows` uses it to restore rows already removed from dup_gating.tsv, anything beyond the cap gets dropped and re-gated on regeneration. I'll now check what the census exposes about stale rows to fix this struct_twins rule bug.

PA3 3.28.6 is installed, and it's a set of small fixes to how updates behave:
- **Installs while paused:** a chosen update can now install while a task is paused and no agent is working. It no longer waits for the task to end.
- **Clearer restart messages:** the status line reads "installing, please wait" until the install has really finished. After a restart I'll say plainly that the new version is loaded, so an old restart line doesn't look like a loop.
- **No double installs:** I now hear only the new version and its title, so I can't start a second install beside the mod's.
- **Windows:** background steps no longer open empty console windows.

**To load it: /exit, then `claude --continue` (not /clear).**

The restart also applies a changed setting and loads the new mod and agents, and you'll pick up where you left off. T3 is at its clean pause point (batch 3 committed), and nothing starts until you're back and say go.

done. T3 is finished: every map type still blocked as "not canonical" now has a decision, and the final verify is green. The cause counts have not moved yet; that waits on the T4/T5 sweeps and restamps. Last commit is 71fe30987b.

**What is in place**
- **Band.** `config/tnc_band.tsv` is frozen from the post-T2 census and matches T2's projection exactly: 6,676 types / 132,544 sites in the band, 6,066 types / 6,976 sites in the tail.
- **Decision mode.** `restruct.py --decide-band` writes every row by rule, none by hand. A 30-decision sample (10 fold, 10 residual, 10 define) checked against struct_map.json was 30/30 correct.
- **Band rows (6,673).**
  - 5,301 define;
  - 18 fold, all on E2 global-anchor evidence; E1 found none;
  - residual: TYPE-NO-LAYOUT 513, TYPE-UNION 399, TYPE-SCALAR 225, TYPE-CANON-TWIN 185 (the class you accepted), TYPE-INTERIOR 32.
- **Tail rows.** 6,065 `TYPE-BELOW-BAND` rows, each with evidence `band:<rank>/<sites>`.
- **Header batches.** The 5,301 defs went into include/struct_types.h in three batches by rank (1-216, 217-1834, 1835-6676). Each batch passed R22 218/218 with check-structs OK and was committed. A fresh census before batches 2 and 3 and before the tail rows found 0 missing names.
- **Restruct.py:401 verdict list.** It needs no new name. TYPE-BELOW-BAND is a site cause, and those bodies keep the verdict TYPE-NOT-CANONICAL, which is already listed.
- **Other tool changes.** `--restamp --restamp-no-edit` is added and covered by the selftest, not run fleet-wide. restruct_cycle.sh now exits 0 with "nothing drawable" on a batch that compiles nothing and writes nothing, proven on one batch.

**Final verify:** check-structs OK (0 dup classes, 0 dead, 738 twins listed, 5,303 dead-kept names until T4/T5); restruct selftest 123/123; clean R22 218/218; tools-health OK.

**Deviations**
- **TYPE-CANON-TWIN** is a fifth residual class beyond the four the plan named.
- **struct_twins.py** was changed, though it is not in the task's file list. It now cites census use sites for the new defs, so their layout twins pass check-structs; the gate itself is unchanged. It also no longer drops listed twins beyond type_census.json's 300-class cap.
- **Corpus refresh.** I re-ran `make kit-corpus` because the Project Architect reinstall left two harness corpus copies stale.

**Costs for T4/T5**
- The header grew from 108 KB to 2.0 MB. A clean R22 now takes about 9 minutes, against 5m38 before.
- `make tools-health` now takes about 15 minutes, and a 900-second timeout kills it.

**Recommendation for T4/T5:** sweep the band with restruct_cycle.sh. After each sweep, run `--residue`, `--restamp` and `--check-residue`, plus `--restamp-no-edit` once. Prune defs drawn in 0 bodies as TYPE-NO-MEMBER and take them off the dead-kept list.

**Final verify:** check-structs OK (0 dup classes, 0 dead, 738 twins listed, 5,303 dead-kept names until T4/T5); restruct selftest 123/123; clean R22 218/218; tools-health OK.

**Deviations**
- **TYPE-CANON-TWIN** is a fifth residual class beyond the four the plan named.
- **struct_twins.py** was changed, though it is not in the task's file list. It now cites census use sites for the new defs, so their layout twins pass check-structs; the gate itself is unchanged. It also no longer drops listed twins beyond type_census.json's 300-class cap.
- **Corpus refresh.** I re-ran `make kit-corpus` because the Project Architect reinstall left two harness corpus copies stale.

**Costs for T4/T5**
- The header grew from 108 KB to 2.0 MB. A clean R22 now takes about 9 minutes, against 5m38 before.
- `make tools-health` now takes about 15 minutes, and a 900-second timeout kills it.

**Recommendation for T4/T5:** sweep the band with restruct_cycle.sh. After each sweep, run `--residue`, `--restamp` and `--check-residue`, plus `--restamp-no-edit` once. Prune defs drawn in 0 bodies as TYPE-NO-MEMBER and take them off the dead-kept list.

## 223751-007r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-16T04:37:51.960Z
session: a4742bc1-12ac-4649-ab77-694e7315677f
agentId: afedb6a208e6edc09
model: claude-opus-5-5[1m]
effort: medium
turnId: 75e25eb1-d23c-4e16-af41-f8ff1aeaa4a6
usage: {input: 6, cacheRead: 102839, cacheWrite: 55381, output: 1936}
---

No, the real game didn't have anything like 12,700 structs. The count is an artifact of how the types were recovered. We are deduplicating as we go, and we're also naming them, but naming comes later, in 3.43.

**Why there are so many.** Whenever the decompiler saw code reading a block of memory at fixed offsets, it made a placeholder struct named after the address where it first saw it: `Unkstruct_80126B58` and so on. Two things multiply that:
- **One struct seen from many places.** A single real struct used in ten places can show up as ten different placeholders, each holding only the few fields that place happened to touch.
- **218 separately compiled binaries.** Most of them are overlays, code loaded per scene or room. Each one sees the same game structs on its own, so the same entity or player block gets found again in each overlay.

The roughly 12,700 types in `config/tnc_band.tsv` are the types that cast sites still point at. A big share of them cover only one site each, which is why I recommended the 95% band rather than a canonical definition for every one. The original source probably had a few hundred real structs, though I can't prove that number. This pattern is normal for a decomp at this stage. Matching comes first and gives you correct code with placeholder types. Folding those down to the real set is the cleanup we're doing now.

**Deduplication, in three layers:**
- **3.38, struct unification (closed).** It merged duplicate definitions of the same struct into one, starting with the big ones: the entity struct, the player block and a third head type. Its gate is `type_census --check` reporting 0 duplicate definitions.
- **During 3.40.** T2 merged 7 of its top 75 types into existing types (recorded in `config/type_folds.tsv`). T3 added the TYPE-CANON-TWIN reason for 153 new types whose layout exactly matched an existing struct, so they point at that struct instead of becoming a copy.
- **What's left.** The latest census still reports 738 "duplicate classes" across 2,504 distinct layouts. I haven't checked exactly what the census counts as a class. If any of them turn out to be the same struct under two names, 3.41's validation gate and the rename work are where they get caught.

**Naming:**
- **3.42** builds the rename tooling: a scanner that proves every reference to a renamed symbol was rewritten, plus a ranking of which structs and functions matter most.
- **3.43** names the top 500 from that ranking. Each name has to cite real evidence: strings, the debug menu, live-RAM observation, community RAM maps. Anything without evidence keeps its `Unkstruct_<addr>` placeholder, because the plan's rule is that unnamed beats wrong.

The order is deliberate. If we named types before merging them, one real struct would end up with 40 different names.

None of this changes the plan, so I've let the queue go on. T4 is sweeping the overlays onto the new canonical types.

## 233052-009r-claude-answer
---
ts: 2026-10-16T05:30:52.112Z

---

Mostly yes. Every phase closes with a docs update that covers what you're asking for. One gap: the lessons aren't yet in the start-a-project guide, so I've put a picker up for that.

**What's already written each phase:**
- **Hindsight.** `docs/retrospective.md` §7 ("After 100%") asks four questions about the readability work, one Gen3 phase at a time: what we believed, what turned out true, what failed, what it cost, and what we'd do sooner. It currently runs through 3.39. Examples so far: casting to structs was assumed to change nothing in the compiled code, but a probe found it was only 91% neutral; most pins turned out to be wrong declarations rather than compiler quirks; and the permuter (the tool that tries random code variations until one compiles to matching bytes) reproduces shapes we already know but never finds new ones.
- **Narrative.** `docs/story.md` §10 tells the same period as a story.
- **The tech-debt chart.** The README timeline (`docs/story-timeline.svg`) plots the leftover debt, the raw cast sites and the levers, phase by phase. T12 in this phase adds an "uncovered" line next to raw casts, so "recorded as residue" can't be mistaken for "debt gone". Here "levers" are the pins and inline asm we use to force matching output.
- **Wiki.** `docs/wiki/Where-the-project-goes-next.md` is updated at each close.

For 3.40, T14 (after the milestone gate) extends all four. It will cover today's lessons too: the 95% band decision, the long tail of one-off types, and the restruct cycle that kept re-running batches where nothing was left to convert.

**The gap.** The guide for doing a decomp smarter from day one, `docs/how-to-ai-decomp/03-bootstrap-order.md`, still covers only the road to 100% matched. The retrospective's own §4 "do sooner" list cites it, and so far the guide holds nothing about reducing the work after 100%. I'd add lessons like these to it:
- give struct types one canonical definition during matching, instead of creating a placeholder struct each time new code is matched;
- fix declarations before reaching for pins;
- track levers and casts on a debt chart from the first phase.

My recommendation is to add that to T14's done-when, since T14 is already editing the retrospective and only needs to carry the conclusions over. It's a small, docs-only change inside the phase and doesn't touch the milestone. It goes in at the next task boundary, so T4 isn't affected. Pick an option in the picker. The queue is moving.

I've queued a change to T14 (the phase-close docs task). It now also carries the after-100% lessons into the from-the-start guide, `/home/musashi/bfm-decomp/docs/how-to-ai-decomp/03-bootstrap-order.md`. That covers the "do sooner" lessons from Phases 3.35 to 3.40 in retrospective §7, in a new section on reducing the work after 100%:

- **Canonical types:** give struct types one agreed definition while functions are being matched, instead of making a placeholder struct every time new code is matched.
- **Declarations first:** fix wrong declarations before reaching for pins.
- **Debt chart:** track levers and casts on a chart from the first phase.

The section points back to §7, and the link check (`doc_links --strict`) must still report zero broken links. The change goes in at the next task boundary, so T4's sweep isn't affected.
